#### Assistant message `ee48ba0f-7e9a-5175-9387-9e424f9917c1`

````
## Assessment

**The released records establish a real investigative lead, an inconclusive enhancement result, and substantially stronger evidence of presence between the two impacts than before the first. They do not establish foreknowledge. They also do not supply a complete evidentiary resolution of the Exxon lead.**

The central gaps are specific: identifying either surveillance van, verifying the recording time, identifying its occupants, and connecting its location to the viewing position. Even a verified early sighting would require a further evidentiary step to establish advance knowledge.

[**:chatgpt-content-reference{index="4"}**](https://chatgpt.com/library?file_id=libfile_733a996aeb048191849c75ef0b10ab1c&account_id=personal) — the complete 13-page report, including chronology, competing explanations, custody analysis, and a prioritized records list.

**Source convention:** S1, S3, S5, and S6 below denote the FBI release sections linked at the end. Page numbers are **one-based PDF pages in those specific copies**; other mirrors paginate differently. I could not access an attachment in this turn, so I independently obtained the released scans. I verified key page images, but did not obtain the original Exxon video, enhanced image files, or underlying Gulf receipt.

## 1. What connects the Exxon records

| Record | What it establishes | What it does not establish |
|---|---|---|
| **September 17, 2001 surveillance collection** | An unnamed business near UMS supplied surveillance covering September 10–11. **S1, p.137.** | The visible record does not identify Exxon, K1966, or AGBH009. It is a possible acquisition link, not a demonstrated one. |
| **September 28 laboratory request** | Newark submitted an **original surveillance tape and twelve color photographs developed from it**. The request identifies Exxon near the subjects’ business and seeks distinctive markings on **two white vans**. It specifies **08:29:00–approximately 08:31:00 on September 11**. **S5, pp.64–66.** | Neither van had thereby been identified as the subjects’ vehicle. The request supplies no clock calibration. |
| **October 1 receipt, documented October 18** | The laboratory cover explicitly references the September 28 communication. It identifies **K1966**, a TDK T-160 VHS tape, with the parenthetical identifier **AGBH009**, and twelve prints, **K1967–K1978**. **S5, pp.69–70.** | It does not provide the full acquisition-to-laboratory custody record or explain AGBH009’s originating numbering system. |
| **October 17 New York memorandum** | Reports a conditional possible match involving Exxon footage and possible support for earlier presence. **Serial 325, p.4.** | It supplies no K1966/AGBH009 identifier, laboratory number, 08:29 timestamp, confirmed occupant identification, or definitive vehicle match. |
| **October 18 examination** | The examiner digitized the best van frames and applied brightness/contrast adjustment, sharpening, and unsharp masking. The improvement was **minimal**. Prints and discs were produced. **S5, p.71.** | The released result states **neither a match nor an exclusion**, and gives no plate reading, occupant identification, distinctive-feature comparison, or clock finding. |

The September 28 → October 1 → October 18 connection is strong because the laboratory cover explicitly references the referral and matches its submission. Connecting the October 17 memorandum to that same specimen is plausible, but the visible records lack a unique exhibit cross-reference. The memorandum also **predates the signed laboratory result**; preliminary communication is possible, but undocumented here. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="0"}

### Enhanced prints, discs, and subsequent custody

The October 18 routing instructions describe:

- Three sets of thirteen Fuji prints and three CD-Rs accompanying the submitted tape and prints to a redacted **Explosives Unit** examiner. **Two of those sets/discs were designated for New York.**
- Two additional thirteen-print sets and two CD-Rs sent to Newark by Federal Express.
- One additional CD-R kept and sent to file.

That totals **five sets—65 physical output prints—and six CD-Rs**. It does not establish six copies of the complete video, or even identical disc contents. Routing through the Explosives Unit is not an explosives finding. The language describes dispatch arrangements, not signed recipient acknowledgments. **S5, p.70.**

Later records provide administrative history without resolving the tape’s fate:

- **July 10, 2003:** outstanding laboratory leads were to be discontinued. **S6, p.53.**
- **February–April 2004:** evidence tracking and retention pending further headquarters input. **S6, pp.59–61.**
- **January–February 2005:** return offers and prospective destruction of unclaimed subjects’ property. **S6, pp.85–89.**

None identifies K1966 or its derivative discs. **These records do not establish that the Exxon tape was destroyed, returned, or remains retained.**

## 2. The timing question: “first” tower versus United aircraft

The October 17 memorandum refers to presence:

> “before the first World Trade Tower was hit by the United Airlines commercial aircraft.”

That wording is internally inconsistent with the impact sequence. Using the Commission’s chronology, **American 11 hit the North Tower at 08:46:40; United 175 hit the South Tower at 09:03:11**. Both interpretations must therefore be tested. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="1"}

| Interpretation | If 08:29–08:31 is accurate | Recorder delay needed to move the interval to/after impact |
|---|---:|---:|
| “First” means the **North Tower/AA11** impact | 15m40s–17m40s before impact | Latest edge reaches impact with **+15m40s**; the entire interval is at/after impact with **+17m40s** |
| “United” means the **South Tower/UA175** impact | 32m11s–34m11s before impact | Latest edge reaches impact with **+32m11s**; the entire interval is at/after impact with **+34m11s** |

Here, “+” means **actual time minus displayed time**: a slow recorder.

These are sensitivity thresholds, **not evidence that the recorder was wrong**. For illustration, a twenty-minute delay would place the recording at 08:49–08:51—after the first impact and before the second. A thirty-five-minute delay would place it after both. No reviewed record establishes either correction.

The referral’s time remains **unverified**, rather than disproved. I found no recorder calibration, contemporaneous clock comparison, station maintenance record, or raw footage allowing independent synchronization. **S5, p.66.**

There is a documented clock error elsewhere: the seized **Canon still camera** displayed the previous evening when examined on September 20. That demonstrates an error in that camera, **not Exxon’s recorder**. **S3, p.72.**

Even with an accurate timestamp and confirmed vehicle match, the footage would establish the vehicle’s presence near Exxon. Establishing presence at **Doric before an impact** requires additional location, travel, and occupant evidence. Presence before United 175’s impact could also be an ordinary response to the earlier American 11 impact.

## 3. Which vehicle and which occupants?

The seized vehicle is identified as **NJ JRJ13Y**, VIN **1GCEG15W4Y1142815**. At the 3:56 p.m. stop, police recorded Sivan Kurzberg as driver, Oded Ellner as front passenger, and Yaron Shmuel/“Shimuel,” Baul/Paul Kurzberg, and Omer Gavriel Marmari among the remaining occupants. The police reports contain spelling and naming inconsistencies, documented in the report. **This afternoon lineup cannot automatically be projected backward onto every sighting.** **S1, p.80; police report, pp.1–4.** :chatgpt-content-reference{index="2"}

The witnesses provide materially different levels of identification:

| Evidence | What it strengthens or weakens |
|---|---|
| **Doric resident interviewed September 11:** three men on a white van after the first fire began and before the second explosion; she contemporaneously wrote **JRJ-13Y** as it left. **S1, pp.63–65.** | Strong vehicle connection to the later stop and affirmative evidence of **between-impacts presence**. She did not establish arrival time or all five occupants’ presence. |
| **September 15 summary:** white van in that lot “as early as 8:00.” **S1, p.33.** | Supports investigating early presence, but the passage supplies no plate or occupant identification. |
| **Lead NK1148, September 15 interview:** approximately **8:15**, a white windowless van backed into the rear lot, facing the skyline. The witness described **red company lettering and “electric supplies,”** no plate, and no visible passengers. **S5, p.29.** | Affirmative evidence of an early van, with a concrete reason to investigate a **different commercial vehicle**. It may be the source of the 8:00 summary; the released cross-reference does not establish that. |
| **Doric painter, interviewed September 12:** three men beside a white utility van, with a brown van farther behind. He **did not recognize the men** in photographs of the five detainees. **S5, p.25.** | A genuine **negative identification result**, limited by unknown viewing and presentation conditions. His “less than five minutes after the first impact” and “9:00–9:10” estimates also conflict. |
| **Exxon referral:** two white vans requiring enhancement. **S5, pp.65–66.** | No released positive identification of either vehicle or its occupants. |
| **UMS employee:** company had one white van, without company lettering on its sides; she recalled magnetic UMS panels in the office. **S3, p.85.** | Modestly narrows company-vehicle alternatives, but does not identify the Exxon vans or eliminate an unrelated electrical-service van. |

The separately reported Liberty State Park vehicle also should not be silently merged into this sequence: the summary gives another location, different descriptive details, and no time. **S1, p.33.**

## 4. Gulf: the morning account and the 2:14 p.m. finding can coexist

The September 14 interview identifies **J&S Gulf, 1324 Willow Avenue, Hoboken**. The attendant said UMS had a gas account and its trucks visited daily, usually in the morning. He recalled a white UMS van that morning and recognized a familiar short-dark-haired driver from prior visits. He did not know the driver’s name, exact visit time, or whether others were inside. **S5, p.31.**

His reason for placing it before the attack was that later customers discussed the event, while this driver did not. Thus:

- Customer recognition and the recalled morning visit are affirmative testimony.
- Placement **before the attack** is an inference from the conversation, not a clock observation.
- Absence of discussion could also occur after an attack, particularly before someone learned of it. **S5, pp.31–32.**

The **2:14 p.m. conclusion was already recorded on September 23, 2001**, not first discovered in the 2003 closing report. Investigators attributed it to witness statements and a Gulf receipt; a late-in-sequence photograph of someone using a squeegee was offered as corroboration. **S5, p.50; repeated in S6, pp.47–48.**

I did not locate the actual September 11 receipt or a transaction-clock audit. The photograph could support an after-attack visit, conditional on its sequence and location; it does not independently supply the exact minute.

**An afternoon purchase does not establish that no morning visit occurred.** The live possibilities include two visits, different UMS users, or an afternoon visit misremembered as morning. An inventory transcribes earlier Gulf receipts dated August 17, August 24, and September 6, all associated with **UMS/JRJ13Y**. Those support ordinary recurring patronage, not two visits on September 11. **S3, p.54.**

Exxon and Gulf are separate stations and separate evidence streams.

## 5. Travel, the timecard, and photographs do not form a fully reconciled itinerary

Three corrections matter:

**The 8:58 timecard was the employee’s.** T-5 said she clocked in then and saw redacted individuals around the dispatch/claims areas. The report does not say two detainees themselves clocked in at 8:58. The follow-up gathered company background rather than testing her earlier timing account—an omitted verification, not a failed corroboration test. **S5, pp.21–22, 52.**

**Five minutes concerns the viewing lot; ten minutes concerns White Glove Movers.** A September 13 subject interview estimates five minutes from UMS to the viewing position. The ten-minute journey later on that page concerns a different destination. Both are recollections, not measured route tests. **S5, pp.85–86.**

**The 7:48 EZ-Pass entry is one location anchor.** The September 23 summary reports passage at the Brooklyn entrance to the Brooklyn–Battery Tunnel. The subject’s account also mentions the Lincoln Tunnel. Without the raw transactions and subsequent movements, this does not establish location during either impact. **S5, pp.51, 85.**

There is a genuine conditional tension: **if the same photographed men were at UMS at a true 8:58, an immediate five-minute trip reaches Doric around 9:03**, conflicting with a photo estimated near 9:00 and the resident’s before-second-impact observation. Identities, clocks, departure time, and travel estimates need reconciliation; none should be adjusted merely to make the story fit.

### Dating a photograph does not date arrival

The detailed helicopter/radar analysis identifies N8BQ as the most likely helicopter, gives an approximately **8:50–9:00** relevant window, and estimates the photograph near **9:00** through smoke/burn comparison. The broader summary instead gives **8:50–9:03**. The detailed document’s cover and continuation also bear different dates. **S5, pp.51, 62–63.**

This supports post-first-impact photography. **It does not exclude arrival before the first impact.** The helicopter reportedly appears in the third photograph; dating that image does not automatically date the preceding two.

Likewise, the closing report’s statement that no recovered still photograph depicts the towers before the attack is a negative finding about the recovered photographic set—not proof that nobody was present earlier. **S6, pp.53–54.**

## 6. Competing explanations and the weight of the evidence

| Explanation | Assessment |
|---|---|
| **Relevant people arrived after the first impact to observe events** | Best supported by the plate-linked between-impacts witness, post-impact photographs, reported office reaction, and photo analysis. The exact itinerary remains unreconciled. |
| **Relevant vehicle or people were present before the first impact without foreknowledge** | Ordinary work travel and fueling provide plausible reasons for nearby presence. The early sightings and Exxon lead leave this worth testing, but do not establish a specific pre-first-impact match. |
| **Relevant people were deliberately positioned with foreknowledge** | A verified early identification plus anticipatory conduct or predictive communications would materially strengthen this. The reviewed evidence does not establish that combination. Perceived celebration does not itself resolve prior knowledge. |
| **Different vehicle, different occupants, or conflated sightings** | Supported as a serious alternative by the electrical-supply lettering, two Exxon vans, unspecified Gulf passengers, and painter’s nonrecognition. The plate-linked Doric sighting nevertheless connects that particular observation to JRJ13Y. |
| **Mistaken timing or identification** | Plausible given inferred Gulf timing, rough recollections, missing recorder calibration, and documentary errors. An error is not proved simply because it would remove a contradiction. |
| **Separate morning and afternoon Gulf visits** | Accommodates both accounts and ordinary patronage. Requires the full transaction record to distinguish it from a single misremembered visit. |

The FBI’s conclusions deserve consideration as investigative outcomes, but not substitution for underlying evidence. Newark’s September 24 assessment was probabilistic and said investigation continued; headquarters used stronger clearance language the same day. Both preceded the September 28 enhancement request and October 17 memorandum. **S5, pp.54–66.**

The July 2003 closing communication reported no corroborated foreknowledge or pre-attack videotaping, but furnished no Exxon-specific comparison or clock resolution. It also confusingly referred to a first hijacked aircraft striking tower #2. **S6, pp.53–57.**

The evidence categories should remain distinct:

- **Affirmative:** submitted tape, documented enhancement and outputs, plate-linked witness observation, earlier Gulf patronage.
- **Negative:** painter’s nonrecognition; September 20 search finding no alleged video camera or evidence of one; no pre-attack scene in the recovered stills. Each has a defined, limited scope.
- **Inconclusive:** minimal enhancement and conditional vehicle resemblance.
- **Missing:** original media, clock audit, actual receipt, identification crosswalks, and item-specific disposition records.

The 2003 repetition of the 2001 Gulf, EZ-Pass, helicopter, and timecard analysis is **not independent corroboration**. **S5, pp.50–52; S6, pp.47–49.**

## 7. Records most likely to discriminate between explanations

1. **The complete laboratory package:** K1966/AGBH009, K1967–K1978, lab **011001250 HC JD / JD**, all output image files and processing notes—especially the **CD-R sent to file**. Compare original frames with NJSP’s documented 76-negative photographic set from the seized-van investigation. **S5, pp.69–71; S1, p.80.**

2. **Exxon acquisition and timing records:** FD-340a envelope, exact station address, camera view, recorder settings, clock comparison, transfer notes, and continuous footage around both impacts. These could validate or defeat the central time-and-identity premise.

3. **The October 17 author’s underlying analysis:** the precise vehicle features, referenced witness, intended impact, interoffice communications, and final lead disposition. This would connect—or separate—Serial 325 and K1966.

4. **Contemporaneous witness and business records:** NK1148 and the underlying 8:00 complaint; the painter’s diagram and identification procedure; Gulf’s complete September 11 account ledger and original receipt; T-5’s actual timecard; raw toll and telephone records. These can distinguish different vehicles, two visits, and incompatible simultaneous locations.

5. **Item-specific custody records:** recipient acknowledgments, evidence inventories, the laboratory file-copy index, and any return or destruction certificate. General property-disposal memoranda cannot answer what happened to this tape.

### Primary FBI source copies

These are public mirrors of the released FBI-origin scans; their hosting sites are not independent corroborating sources.

| Explicit Item H cross-reference | **S3, p.51; handwritten attachment p.7** | Identifies an insurance-card copy also present in Item H. This joins the **insurance-card information**, not the separate receipts. |

Sources: [S1 inventory](https://ia601906.us.archive.org/11/items/FBIandPoliceReportUrbanMoving/dancing-israelis-fbi-document-section-1-1138796-001-303a-nk-105536-section-1-944861.pdf#page=91) and [S3 inventory](https://ia601906.us.archive.org/11/items/FBIandPoliceReportUrbanMoving/FBI-document-Section-3-1138796-001-303A-NK-105536-Section-3-944880.pdf#page=43).

These entries affirmatively support **routine business between this van/company and Gulf**. They cannot supply the missing September 11 transaction.

The reported squeegee photograph could corroborate an after-attack stop if its location and film sequence are established. Its position late in a sequence would not independently establish **14:14**, and an afternoon photograph would not exclude a separate morning purchase.

The competing explanations therefore remain distinguishable: two visits by the same van; different UMS drivers or vehicles; an afternoon visit remembered as morning; or an error in interpreting the receipt. **The original transaction and full daily/account ledger would discriminate among them.** The summary alone does not.

## Exxon: examination documented, identification outcome still missing

The strongest documentary chain is:

- **September 28 referral — S5, pp.64–66:** one original surveillance tape and twelve derived color prints enclosed in an **FD-340a envelope**. The request concerns distinctive markings on **two white vans** and reports a tape interval of **08:29:00–approximately 08:31:00**.
- **October 1 receipt, documented October 18 — S5, pp.69–70:** laboratory report expressly references the September 28 communication. **K1966** is a TDK T-160 VHS, with **AGBH009** in parentheses; **K1967–K1978** are the twelve submitted prints.
- **October 18 examination — S5, p.71:** best frames were digitized and processed. Improvement was minimal. The report does **not state a vehicle match, exclusion, or formal inconclusive comparison**. [Referral and laboratory packet](https://ia601906.us.archive.org/11/items/FBIandPoliceReportUrbanMoving/fbi-document-section-5-62394321-1138796-001-303a-nk-105536-section-5-944891.pdf#page=64).

**October 17 Serial 325, p.4** describes a conditional possible match from an Exxon tape in Weehawken. It does not supply the specimen identifiers, laboratory number, recorder interval, distinctive features, or the identity of the witness it might support. The subject matter strongly suggests a related lead, but the unique-identifier connection is not exposed. Its date preceding the signed enhancement report leaves an intermediate viewing or communication possible—not demonstrated. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="1"}

### What the routing establishes

**S5, p.70** accounts for **five sets of thirteen Fuji prints and six CD-Rs**:

- Three sets/discs, the tape, and submitted prints went through an Explosives Unit examiner; **two of those sets/discs were designated for New York**.
- Two additional sets/discs were sent to Newark by FedEx.
- One CD-R was retained/sent to file.

These are production and routing statements. They do not prove delivery, thirteen distinct moments, six full-video copies, or a later identification result.

I found **no subsequent K1966-specific receipt, return, destruction certificate, or final comparison worksheet**. The 2005 correspondence concerns abandoned personal property and prospective destruction; it does not identify the station-supplied tape or its derivatives. **S6, pp.85–89.**

## The clock question remains unresolved

The **08:29–08:31 interval is the FBI’s reported tape time**. Without the footage, I cannot independently inspect its overlay. No contemporaneous comparison with actual time, clock-setting history, recorder model, or calibration worksheet was located.

The October 17 memo’s wording combines **“first” tower** with a **United aircraft**. Those descriptions point to different events: American 11 struck the North Tower at **08:46:40**; United 175 struck the South Tower at **09:03:11**. Both interpretations must therefore be tested. **Serial 325, p.4; 9/11 Commission Report, printed pp.7–8.** :chatgpt-content-reference{index="2"}

Let **δ = actual time minus reported tape time**, positive when the recorder was slow:

| Interpretation | Entire 08:29–08:31 interval precedes impact if | Earliest frame is at/after impact if |
|---|---:|---:|
| First/North impact, 08:46:40 | δ < **15m 40s** | δ ≥ **17m 40s** |
| United/South impact, 09:03:11 | δ < **32m 11s** | δ ≥ **34m 11s** |

Offsets between each pair make the interval reach or cross the impact. A hypothetical **+20 minutes** yields 08:49–08:51, between impacts; **+35 minutes** yields 09:04–09:06, after both. **These are sensitivity calculations, not measured errors.**

Even a synchronized pre-impact Exxon stop would still need a vehicle/occupant identification and an established route to support pre-impact presence at the viewing location.

### New timing records—and their limits

**S1, pp.43–47** contains an actual FBI transcription of stored data from three seized phones. The Samsung introduced on p.44 has, on **p.45/printed p.3**, an outgoing **09/11 09:01A** entry and incoming **09/11 02:14P** entry. The latter is **a telephone call**, not the Gulf receipt. The former is a potentially useful record to compare with the reported en-route UMS call, but the owner, recipient, location and purpose are not joined in the disclosed material. No handset-clock audit or carrier-certified timing was located. [Phone extraction](https://ia601906.us.archive.org/11/items/FBIandPoliceReportUrbanMoving/dancing-israelis-fbi-document-section-1-1138796-001-303a-nk-105536-section-1-944861.pdf#page=43).

**S5, pp.1–5** documents a September 17 attempt to obtain employee-arrival/timecard information. The office was locked. This corrects any implication that investigators never attempted the inquiry, but supplies no actual card or clock test. T-5’s reported **8:58** remains an unverified card time. **S5, pp.21–22.**

Travel does not yet resolve the conflict: the subject’s estimated **five-minute UMS-to-viewing-lot trip** would put someone leaving at a true 8:58 there around 9:03, creating tension with the photograph estimate near 9:00. Identity, clocks and rough estimates remain unaligned. The **ten-minute** estimate in that interview concerns **White Glove Movers**, not the viewing lot. **S5, pp.51, 62–63, 85–86.**

## Photographs, later records, and result classifications

I inspected the separately circulated **17-page photograph release**. Pages 4–17 are image-bearing pages, including repeated or split views—not established as fourteen distinct negatives. They do **not supply the Exxon enhancement set, a van-comparison plate, or the Gulf squeegee image**. Its first surviving letter page lacks the request header, preventing a firm FOIA-number crosswalk. [Photograph PDF](https://ia601600.us.archive.org/15/items/DancingIsraelisHighFivers/409691150-FOIA-Release-of-9-11-Dancing-Israelis-thru-the-FBI.pdf).

A different record, **S1, p.80**, documents receipt from New Jersey State Police of three copies each of **76 black-and-white photographs plus negatives** concerning the van investigation. It does not establish that these were 76 photographs from a detainee’s personal film. The actual comparison views remain unretrieved.

The correct **NY Serial 337**, **PDF pp.4–5/printed pp.5–6**, adds a reported separate **car** journey to a Hoboken storage facility before 9, presence there at the first impact, and later assembly of five people in a van. Redactions and absent independent timing prevent treating this as verified, but it reinforces the need to distinguish morning occupants and trips. One archive’s file bearing this serial’s name is unrelated; the correct release is six PDF pages, including a deletion sheet. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="3"}

The relevant categories are:

| Category | Located example |
|---|---|
| **Affirmative evidence** | Tape submission, examination and routing; plate-linked testimony; recorded accounts and receipt inventories. |
| **Negative identification result** | Painter did not recognize the five men shown in photographs. **S5, p.25.** This does not establish their absence. |
| **Limited technical result** | Minimal improvement from Exxon enhancement. **S5, p.71.** |
| **Missing result** | Final Exxon vehicle comparison. It cannot be relabeled an exclusion or a formally inconclusive examination. |

Later polygraph reporting is a separate evidentiary process. **NY368, pp.1–2** records examiner interpretations and HQ concurrence and identifies a **Lafayette polygraph disk**—not an Exxon CD-R. The underlying charts are not exposed in that two-page release. **NY459** exposes only its cover and a sheet identifying original pp.2–4 as withheld. Neither supplies a retrievable Exxon result. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="4"}

## Search limits and what would change the assessment

The audit covered the six-part release’s **579 PDF pages**, including visual inspection of all **95 low-OCR pages**; four archive inventories; the separately released photographs; additional New York serials; and a 22 MB website archive. Exact searches included **K1966, AGBH009, 011001250**, Gulf receipt/ledger variants, and clock/timecard terms.

No sought original media or receipt surfaced. **MuckRock’s direct listing/API returned HTTP 403**, so that route remains an access failure, not an established empty collection. A large video compilation lacking identified K1966 provenance was not viewed. The report records these boundaries.

The highest-value missing evidence is **the tape plus comparison packet; a recorder-time anchor; and the Gulf receipt, ledger, photograph sequence and attendant follow-up**. Witness identity crosswalks, carrier records and attendance records could then test travel and occupancy.

The available evidence supports **JRJ13Y’s presence between impacts more directly than its presence before the first impact**. Earlier presence remains a testable possibility, including ordinary business activity, different vehicles or occupants, and timing error. **A confirmed pre-impact presence would still not, by itself, establish foreknowledge.**
````

````
**The known recordings were collected, withheld for years, and released in stages.** There are documented disclosure failures and unresolved questions about the originals. I found **no authenticated missile footage or verified order to destroy a clearer impact recording**. The broader recorded evidence supports American Airlines Flight 77, a Boeing 757. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="0"}

I put the detailed investigation, document excerpts, limitations, and 22 linked sources into this eight-page report: **:chatgpt-content-reference{index="12"}[pentagon_video_evidence_research.pdf](sandbox:/workspace/scratch/e68d27dd27be/output/pdf/pentagon_video_evidence_research.pdf)**.

## What happened to the known footage

| Recording | What the evidence establishes |
|---|---|
| **Pentagon checkpoint cameras** | Two views were publicly released on **May 16, 2006**. The federal court still hosts a downloadable compilation and twelve still images. I inspected the two views; they provide a poor view of the incoming object. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="1"} |
| **Citgo / Navy Exchange gas station** | Released in **September 2006**. The FBI’s forensic video unit reported that it did not show the impact. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="2"} |
| **Doubletree Hotel** | Public by **December 2, 2006**. Contemporary CNN reporting described an explosion but no incoming aircraft. Judicial Watch subsequently reported receiving **37 hours from ten cameras**. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="3"} |
| **Sheraton National Hotel** | The FBI reported that its search could not locate a recording. That does not establish whether one never existed, was misplaced, or was destroyed. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="4"} |
| **Virginia traffic cameras** | VDOT footage was requested. I could not verify camera-specific recording, collection, or disposition records. A request naming cameras does not establish that they recorded the impact. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="5"} |

## The “85 videos” claim needs careful interpretation

The FBI identified **85 potentially responsive recordings**, according to the court’s account of agent Jacqueline Maguire’s declaration:

- **56** were excluded using custody records and supporting documentation.
- She personally reviewed **29**: **16** did not show the crash site, **12** showed aftermath, and **one** was identified as showing the impact.

That was an evidence-search pool, not 85 confirmed views of the collision. The court described the responsive record as a **CD-ROM containing images from security cameras**, so “one record” can encompass multiple camera views. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="6"}

A related detail matters: the Citgo supervisor who reported that agents took the film also said **he had never seen what the pictures showed**. His statement supports reported collection, not a claim that he personally watched clear impact footage. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="7"}

## Where the public accounting remains incomplete

The most concrete custody document I found is a **September 12, 2001 FBI receipt**, reproduced on page 5 of the report. It lists **16 surveillance tapes and a digital recording unit** received from Pentagon protective services. A Commission document index corroborates the receipt’s identifiers, **NCT0073581–82**. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="8"}

I could not locate a complete public reconciliation connecting those items to the later 85-record inventory, published copies, and their present disposition. **That is a specific, investigable records gap.**

The stated withholding basis was protection of law-enforcement proceedings associated with the Moussaoui prosecution. The appellate record also says Judicial Watch’s appeal was ignored for more than a year; releases eventually occurred under court-enforced agreements. Those delays warrant scrutiny. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="9"}

On the aircraft itself, the NTSB reconstructed Flight 77’s route using **radar and a flight-data recorder recovered at the scene**. That evidence supports the airliner identification independently of the blurry CCTV. The footage’s poor quality limits what can be identified visually; it does not supply affirmative evidence of a missile. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="10"}

## 1. Saudi-linked assistance and the proposed UMS connection

**Omar al-Bayoumi’s help settling Hazmi and Mihdhar in San Diego is documented.** The evidence for Fahad al-Thumairy’s involvement is more disputed, although a federal judge found it persuasive at the jurisdictional stage in 2025. I found no public, attributable call, payment, correspondence, or job record connecting either man, the other leads below, or the two hijackers to Urban Moving Systems, Max Movers, or their personnel. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="0"}

| Person or lead | What the public record supports |
|---|---|
| **Omar al-Bayoumi** | After the reported February 1, 2000 restaurant encounter, the two men sought him out on February 4. He also organized a welcome gathering that Khalid Bin Don videotaped. Bin Don’s April 20, 2004 FBI interview supports the chance-encounter account. Judge Daniels later drew a more adverse inference from the broader evidence. | **Fahad al-Thumairy** | The Saudi consular official and King Fahad Mosque imam had documented calls with Bayoumi. Their contents remain unknown. The 2004 Commission found no evidence he assisted the pair. In 2025, Daniels credited Johar’s testimony about an early mosque meeting and Zeid and Alzamari’s testimony about a June 9, 2000 encounter. The judge also cited an FBI-held photograph. I did not independently authenticate that photograph or recover the full depositions. ECF 11182, p. 41, cites plaintiffs’ exhibits 12A, 101, 116 and 117. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="2"} |
| **Musaed al-Jarrah, Adel al-Sadhan and Mutaib al-Sudairy** | Al-Jarrah headed Islamic Affairs at the Saudi embassy. An FBI summary records embassy-number contacts with Thumairy and Bayoumi and raises possible tasking by al-Jarrah. The released material does not supply the conversations or a tasking order. The FBI also considered whether al-Sadhan and al-Sudairy were an advance team. Its overview records no indication of direct contact with the pair, and a February 7, 2023 court order found the showing insufficient to reopen discovery on that theory. | **Other direct helpers and contacts** | Saudi national **Omar Bakarbashat** acknowledged helping Hazmi with English and taking over the pair’s first apartment. **Yazeed al-Salmi** bought travelers checks in Riyadh, of which Hazmi deposited $1,900 on September 5, 2000 after withdrawing the same amount in cash. The purpose remains unclear. **Mohdar Abdullah** translated, helped with licensing and school applications, and drove the pair to Los Angeles on June 9. The FBI recorded conflicting accounts of his alleged jailhouse admissions. **Abdussattar Shaikh**, an FBI source, rented them a room. **Anwar al-Awlaki** acknowledged meeting Hazmi in San Diego. The Commission suspected he may have directed **Eyad al-Rababah** to help Hazmi and Hani Hanjour in Virginia in 2001. Rababah described a chance meeting. That episode concerns a different pair. [Commission Report, ch. 7](https://911commission.gov/report/911Report_Ch7.htm), especially pp. 218–222 and 229–230 · FBI July 16, 2004 EC, EO14040-000852–000858. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="4"} |
| **Osama Bassnan, Princess Haifa and Prince Bandar** | Payments to Bassnan’s household and the later intelligence-source account about Bandar are separate leads. The Commission’s June 16, 2004 hearing found no evidence that the charitable funds reached the conspiracy. The FBI’s June 14, 2017 *Albayoumi / GIP Cooptee* communication records a source account that Bayoumi received a monthly Saudi intelligence stipend through Bandar. The visible record does not establish the source’s access, document the payments, or show CIA direction. [Senate hearing and FBI exhibit, printed pp. 55–60](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CHRG-118shrg53703/pdf/CHRG-118shrg53703.pdf), EO14040-002638–002643. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="5"} |

**The FBI closure and the civil ruling answer different questions.** On May 27, 2021, the FBI and federal prosecutors concluded there was insufficient evidence to prosecute Bayoumi, Thumairy or al-Jarrah for knowingly conspiring to assist the hijackers in furtherance of the attack. On August 28, 2025, Daniels found the evidence sufficient to overcome Saudi Arabia’s immunity challenge, drawing preliminary inferences about coordination, scope of employment and deliberate indifference. The order allowed the civil case to proceed. It did not adjudicate liability. The clipped visitor-card receipt has no verified issuance or entry transaction. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="13"}

## 3. Dogs, patrols, security and power

**September 6 and September 11 refer to different details in the earliest reporting I located.** The *Los Angeles Times*’ September 12, 2001 wire brief, *Security Alert Was Lifted Only Days Ago*, says bomb-sniffing dogs were removed and quotes Tower One guard Daria Coard saying, “Today [Tuesday] was the first day there was not the extra security.” The brief gives no date for the dogs’ removal. A preserved copy of Curtis L. Taylor and Sean Gardiner’s September 12 *Newsday* report says “on Thursday,” which dates that reported change to **September 6**. Both accounts describe two weeks of 12-hour shifts following phone threats. They share the same named witness. *Loose Change*, second-edition transcript, places the dogs’ removal **and** the end of the extended shifts under September 6, compressing the distinction in the reporting. I located the transcript and a later copy of the *Newsday* text. I did not verify an original print page or a film timecode. [LA Times archive](https://www.latimes.com/archives/la-xpm-2001-sep-12-mn-44946-story.html) · [Preserved Newsday text](https://groups.google.com/g/alt.sports.football.pro.ne-patriots/c/_MAllntkkf0) · [Second-edition transcript, September 6 entry](https://www.scripts.com/script/loose_change_2nd_edition_12812). :chatgpt-content-reference{index="14"}

**The claim that every explosives-detection dog was removed is contradicted.** Port Authority Police officer David Lim told the Commission on March 31, 2003 that his September 11 assignment was checking trucks for explosives and that he and his explosives-detection dog Sirius were in the B‑1 kennel area of 2 WTC around 08:30. Contemporary April 2002 reporting also documents Sirius’s WTC assignment. That evidence leaves open how many supplemental teams or patrols were withdrawn, which posts changed, and on what dates. Port Authority official Alan Reiss’s May 18, 2004 testimony describes ordinary access controls and the July 2001 management transition, including Silverstein’s own security director. The alert cancellation, threat log, K‑9 rosters, guard overtime sheets and post orders for September 6–11 did not surface. Those records could distinguish Port Authority police coverage from private guard staffing and identify any actual gaps. [Lim statement](https://9-11commission.gov/hearings/hearing1/witness_lim.htm) · [April 25, 2002 LA Times](https://www.latimes.com/archives/la-xpm-2002-apr-25-mn-39888-story.html) · [Reiss statement, pp. 3–4 and 8–9](https://www.9-11commission.gov/hearings/hearing11/reiss_statement.pdf). :chatgpt-content-reference{index="15"}

**The September 8–9 power-down claim rests on a later tenant account.** The earliest dated version I located is a public reproduction of Scott Forbes’s **April 19, 2004** email. It describes roughly **36 hours above floor 50 in the South Tower**, a tenant IT shutdown and restart, and cabling work as the explanation he was given. The claimed effects on cameras and locks extend beyond the tenant systems he describes working on. A July 20, 2007 posting about his July 18 interview gives **Saturday noon to Sunday 2 p.m.**, or 26 hours, and reports a Port Authority denial. I did not locate the original denial or a switching order. The installer described redundant security networks and emergency generators, which makes the effect of any tenant power outage on particular cameras or locks a separate question. Dated switching orders, tenant notices, contractor sign-ins, and alarm, CCTV or access-control logs would test the outage’s scope and its claimed security effects. [2004 email reproduction](https://www.serendipity.li/wot/forbes01.htm) · [2007 interview notice](https://www.indybay.org/newsitems/2007/07/20/18436524.php) · [January 2001 installer account](https://www.ecmweb.com/content/article/20889492/towering-security). :chatgpt-content-reference{index="16"}

## Search boundaries

- **Saudi links.** I checked the Commission, inspectors general, FBI EO 14040 releases, congressional exhibits, public court materials and contemporary reporting. I cross-checked names and spelling variants against UMS, Max Movers, Suter, Classic and the released FBI comparisons. Redacted database hits and missing attachments limit the negative finding. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="17"}
- **Footage.** I traced the Newark, New York and Philadelphia leads by serial, specimen and lab number and checked NIST and museum catalog leads. Some archive downloads were inaccessible in this pass, so the detailed Newark scan pagination also draws on the earlier public-source review. This was a records and catalog search, without a frame-by-frame audit of every broadcast or recovery of the Exxon media. 
- **Security and power.** I checked contemporary press variants, the second-edition transcript, Commission testimony, NARA’s public finding aid and indexed Port Authority material. I found no date-specific operational records resolving the supplemental alert or alleged shutdown. The New York litigation commonly surfaced in those searches concerns the **1993 bombing** and cannot establish September 2001 staffing. :chatgpt-content-reference{index="19"}

No contacts, records requests, payments or disclosures were made.

# Intelligence withholding hypothesis: January 2000–September 2001

## Executive assessment

The available evidence establishes a narrower but significant proposition: **CIA personnel deliberately stopped at least one proposed transmission of information about Khalid al-Mihdhar to the FBI in January 2000.** The information included his U.S. visa. The report was not transmitted, and subsequent investigations could not corroborate an internal CIA assertion that the relevant travel documents had already been provided to the FBI.

This is affirmative evidence of an intentional information-sharing interruption. It is not, by itself, proof of the purpose behind that interruption.

The central hypothesis contains four distinct propositions\:

1. Identifiable officials possessed actionable information about Mihdhar and Nawaf al-Hazmi.
2. Officials knowingly prevented FBI personnel from receiving information they otherwise would have received.
3. They did so to protect an ongoing intelligence operation.
4. That decision materially contributed to the two men remaining unidentified or unlocated by relevant U.S. investigators until September 2001.

**The first proposition is established for specific information and officials. The second is established for the January 2000 hold, but not for every subsequent failure to share. The third remains unproved. The fourth is plausible and supported by missed investigative opportunities, but its precise causal contribution is not established.**

The evidence does not establish that the officials responsible knew the specific September 11 plot, intended the attacks to succeed, or represented a unified institutional decision.

The most important distinction is between **intentional withholding of a message** and **intentional protection of a covert operation**\. The former is documented; the latter requires additional evidence about instructions, operational objectives, and the decision-makers' knowledge.

Primary starting sources\:

- urlDOJ Inspector General, *A Review of the FBI's Handling of Intelligence Information Related to the September 11 Attacks* \(June 2006), Chapter Five, pp. 240–243, 250https://oig.justice.gov/sites/default/files/legacy/special/s0606/final.pdf\.
- urlCIA Inspector General, *Accountability With Respect to the 9/11 Attacks*, pp. 53–55https://www.cia.gov/readingroom/docs/DOC_0006184107.pdf\.
- url9/11 Commission, *Final Report*, Chapter 6 and Chapter 8https://www.9-11commission.gov/report/911Report.pdf\.

These sources provide overlapping but not wholly independent reconstructions. Both inspectors general relied partly on agency records and retrospective interviews.

## 1\. Precise chronology and knowledge distribution

The following distinguishes what an official or investigator could have known at the time from what later investigations reconstructed.

| Date | Information and identifiable actors | Ability to act and evidentiary significance |
|--------|--------|--------|
| **December 1999–January 4, 2000** | NSA-derived reporting identified travel associated with an al-Qaeda meeting in Malaysia. Material reached CIA counterterrorism personnel and FBI headquarters, including threat reporting. | An investigative lead existed, but not yet the full U.S. travel picture. DOJ OIG, Ch. V, pp. 233–238. |
| **January 5** | CIA personnel obtained Mihdhar's passport information, including a multiple-entry U.S. visa. The officer called **Michelle** in the DOJ report followed the information; **John**, the unit deputy chief, accessed relevant reporting. | This was actionable for watchlisting, immigration checks and FBI referral, even without knowledge of an attack plan. DOJ OIG, pp. 238–242. |
| **January 5, approximately 9:30 a.m.** | **Dwight**, an FBI agent detailed to CIA, drafted a Central Intelligence Report (CIR) containing Mihdhar's visa information, intended New York destination and surveillance information. | An actual proposed dissemination mechanism existed. This is stronger than a retrospective claim that someone merely should have shared information. |
| **January 5, approximately 4 p.m.** | Michelle placed the CIR on hold, attributing the instruction to John. | A deliberate interruption is documented. The instruction's ultimate reason is not. |
| **January 5, later that day** | Michelle's internal cable stated that the passport and visa documents had already been passed to the FBI. | Strong counterevidence to a simple narrative of universal concealment, but the claimed transmission lacks corroborating recipient, routing or receipt evidence. |
| **January 5–6** | CIA detailee **James** discussed the Malaysia information with FBI personnel **Bob** and **Ted**\. FBI threat reporting contained portions of the CIA reporting. | Information was not wholly withheld. The surviving account does not establish transmission of the visa information. |
| **January 8–15** | Mihdhar and Hazmi traveled through Bangkok and entered Los Angeles on January 15. | Their entry created an opportunity for domestic investigation. The CIA did not yet have all the information later reconstructed. |
| **January 13** | The CIA OIG identifies a follow-up concerning the Malaysia travelers that was not adequately pursued. | Relevant to whether the failure was sustained neglect or intentional suppression; the original follow-up record requires direct examination. |
| **Early March 2000** | CIA Bangkok reported that Hazmi had flown to Los Angeles on January 15. Another recipient acknowledged the information with interest. | No contemporaneous FBI dissemination or U.S. watchlisting was established. This is a second major failure, distinct from the January hold. |
| **February–June 2000** | Bayoumi assisted Mihdhar and Hazmi in San Diego. They subsequently lived in the home of FBI informant Abdussattar Shaikh. | The men were accessible through ordinary domestic investigative methods, but the San Diego FBI personnel did not have the critical identifying intelligence. |
| **Late 2000–January 2001** | Intelligence from a joint FBI/CIA source connected the Malaysia meeting to the USS *Cole* investigation and Khallad. | The significance of the earlier information increased. Investigators had a renewed reason to connect the identities and photographs. |
| **May–June 11, 2001** | CIA and FBI personnel discussed the Malaysia meeting and Cole investigation. Some information was not passed to the New York criminal investigators. | The intelligence/criminal-investigation separation was invoked, but whether particular nondisclosures reflected policy, misunderstanding or another purpose requires individual examination. |
| **July 4, 2001** | Mihdhar reentered the United States at JFK using his own identity. | The absence of an effective watchlist entry eliminated a potential interception opportunity. |
| **August 21–24** | FBI analyst **Mary** located the earlier travel reporting and informed FBI headquarters employee **Donna**\. Immigration checks confirmed Mihdhar's July entry. Watchlisting action followed. | Demonstrates that some personnel actively worked to resolve the lead once they recognized its importance. |
| **August 29–September 11** | The FBI opened a search for Mihdhar, but assigned insufficient urgency and restricted involvement by certain New York criminal investigators. | This was a separate FBI prioritization and legal-policy failure after the CIA information became available. |

The dates and actor labels above follow DOJ OIG Chapter V, particularly pp. 238–255, 310–350, and CIA OIG pp. 53–55. The March 2000, June 2001 and August 2001 chronology is also described in the 9/11 Commission's Chapters 6 and 8. citeturn246203search0turn246203search1turn246203search29

An important correction to overly broad withholding accounts: **the FBI did receive some information in January 2000**\. Its January 5 threat update incorporated portions of the Malaysia reporting, and James documented his FBI briefing. However, the DOJ OIG found no corroboration that the U.S. visa information reached FBI personnel. The distinction is central to assessing whether someone selectively withheld the most domestically actionable information.

The DOJ OIG also identified a separate FBI failure: the limited information received was not entered into a useful, searchable investigative system. That is evidence for a mixed explanation rather than one exclusively attributable to CIA decisions. citeturn684075search5turn684075search34

## 2\. Evidence matrix

Status describes the underlying evidence, not the truth of every inference drawn from it.

| Item | Evidence and provenance | Status | Effect on hypotheses |
|--------|--------|--------|--------|
| **E1 — January CIR hold** | CIA system records reconstructed by DOJ and CIA inspectors general; DOJ pp. 240–243 | Documented | Strong for intentional interruption; motive unresolved |
| **E2 — Claimed FBI transmission** | Michelle's contemporaneous CIA cable; DOJ pp. 242–243 | Documented assertion, uncorroborated event | Challenges simple concealment account; could reflect mistaken belief or inaccurate reporting |
| **E3 — FBI briefing** | January 5–6 threat update and James's contemporaneous emails; DOJ pp. 244–251 | Documented | Disconfirms total information blackout |
| **E4 — March Hazmi travel** | CIA Bangkok reporting and subsequent agency review; DOJ pp. 251–255 | Documented | Strong evidence of another missed opportunity, not proof of another deliberate hold |
| **E5 — June 2001 Cole discussions** | Agency communications and retrospective interviews; DOJ pp. 310–330 | Substantially documented; disputed recollections | Compatible with compartmentation, source protection and selective obstruction |
| **E6 — Bayoumi assistance** | FBI investigation and subsequent litigation evidence | Documented association and assistance | Supports investigation of an operational relationship, not CIA tasking by itself |
| **E7 — Bayoumi Saudi intelligence relationship** | FBI June 14, 2017 memorandum, *Albayoumi / GIP Cooptee*, EO14040-002638–002639 | Official intelligence assessment; underlying source redacted | Strengthens Saudi-intelligence component; does not establish CIA control |
| **E8 — CIA recruitment allegations** | Canestraro declaration, July 20, 2021, pp. 4–5, 20–21 | Attributed confidential-source allegations | Directly supports proposed motive if independently verified; presently uncorroborated at operational-record level |
| **E9 — FBI 2021 closure** | Operation Encore closure assessment, May 27, 2021, pp. 9–11 | Official investigative conclusion | Disconfirms a demonstrated prosecutable Saudi conspiracy; not a direct determination of the January hold's motive |
| **E10 — 2025 civil ruling** | *In re Terrorist Attacks on September 11, 2001*, ECF 11182, August 28, 2025 | Preliminary judicial findings under jurisdictional standards | Supports serious consideration of Saudi assistance evidence; does not establish CIA-directed recruitment |

Primary records for E7–E10\:

- urlFBI 2017 memorandum reproduced in the September 10, 2023 Senate letterhttps://www.hsgac.senate.gov/wp-content/uploads/2023-09-10-Blumenthal-and-Johnson-Letter-to-DOJ-and-FBI-re-Sept.-11.pdf\.
- urlCanestraro declaration, pp. 20–21https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/b/b4/Canestraro_Declaration_%282021%29.pdf\.
- urlFBI Executive Order 14040 release, 2021 closure and addendumhttps://vault.fbi.gov/9-11-attacks-investigation-and-related-materials/9-11-material-released-in-response-to-executive-order-14040/documents-responsive-to-executive-order-14040-section-2-b-ii-part-01-of-01/at_download/file\.
- urlU.S. District Court, ECF 11182, August 28, 2025https://wp.nysd.uscourts.gov/sites/default/files/2025-08/03-md-01570%2011182.pdf\.

The recruitment allegations deserve scrutiny precisely because they propose a mechanism connecting the January information hold to the San Diego support network. Canestraro's CS-23 alleged CIA operational files, instructions through Saudi liaison personnel, and a recruitment attempt involving Bayoumi and Thumairy. But the publicly available declaration is an account of what a confidential source told the investigator; it does not contain the alleged tasking orders or operational files. citeturn684075search1turn684075search2turn684075search0

## 3\. Competing hypotheses

**H1 — Deliberate withholding to protect recruitment or another ongoing operation** (Unproved motive)

**Strongest corroboration\:** The January hold, its attribution to a supervisor, the subsequent absence of the visa information from FBI records, and confidential-source allegations of an attempted recruitment operation. The 2017 Bayoumi memorandum makes the proposed Saudi-intelligence intermediary more concrete.

**Strongest disconfirmation\:** Some Malaysia intelligence was shared; an internal CIA cable asserted that the visa information had been passed; and no authenticated CIA recruitment instruction or corresponding operational file was recovered. The people interviewed by the inspectors general did not acknowledge the alleged motive.

**Assessment\:** A serious, testable hypothesis, but the crucial causal link between the hold and an operation is missing.

---

**H2 — Compartmentation and protection of intelligence sources** (Plausible)

**Strongest corroboration\:** The Malaysia surveillance involved foreign liaison relationships, sensitive collection and compartmented reporting. The later Cole discussions involved restrictions on transferring intelligence to criminal investigators.

**Strongest disconfirmation\:** The January CIR was specifically drafted for FBI dissemination, and its proposed contents included basic visa information. No recovered instruction explains why that information could not be shared with suitable handling restrictions. The January hold and June 2001 restrictions also involved different circumstances.

**Assessment\:** Could explain selected decisions, but a general appeal to source protection is insufficient without identifying the source at risk and the decision-maker's reasoning.

---

**H3 — Mistakes, institutional fragmentation and bureaucratic breakdown** (Well supported)

**Strongest corroboration\:** Incomplete FBI records, inadequate supervision of detailees, poor tracking of intelligence dissemination, missed watchlisting opportunities, failure to connect the Cole investigation, and insufficient priority during the August search. DOJ OIG identified these as concrete deficiencies.

**Strongest disconfirmation\:** An explicit instruction to hold a prepared report cannot be characterized as a purely accidental transmission failure. Multiple missed opportunities also warrant individual explanations rather than being collectively attributed to bureaucracy.

**Assessment\:** Strongly supported as a contributing explanation. It does not fully explain why the January hold was ordered.

---

**H4 — Deliberate withholding for another intelligence purpose** (Unresolved)

**Strongest corroboration\:** The hold may have served an operational objective unrelated to recruitment—for example, preserving surveillance, developing a foreign-intelligence lead, or awaiting confirmation of identities and collection.

**Strongest disconfirmation\:** No authenticated record establishes such an objective or shows that the responsible officials believed delaying FBI notification would advance it.

**Assessment\:** An important alternative to recruitment. Intentional nondisclosure does not uniquely identify its purpose.

The hypotheses are not mutually exclusive. A supervisor might have intended to protect sensitive collection, while other personnel assumed dissemination had already occurred, and subsequent FBI failures compounded the original decision.

This mixed explanation is consistent with the established record, although it too remains partly inferential.

## 4\. Individual responsibility and source independence

The strongest documentary attribution concerns **Michelle's recorded hold instruction and its attribution to John**\. The inspector general's labels are pseudonyms, and this assessment does not independently establish the real identities behind them.

The public allegations naming Richard Blee and Tom Wilshire require a separate evidentiary step: matching each person to a particular instruction, document, approval authority and date. A witness's retrospective identification cannot simply be substituted for the pseudonyms in the inspector general's reconstruction. Canestraro's declaration attributes a withholding allegation to CS-16 and a recruitment account to CS-23, but their underlying knowledge and independence have not been established. citeturn684075search1

There are three particularly important source-independence problems.

First, the 9/11 Commission, CIA OIG and DOJ OIG reviewed overlapping agency records and witnesses. Agreement between their narratives is valuable, but it is not equivalent to three independent contemporary witnesses.

Second, multiple former FBI personnel cited in Canestraro's declaration may have relied on common post-attack investigative information. Their accounts should be compared against the underlying files, not counted as independent confirmation simply because they appear in separate paragraphs.

Third, the 2017 Bayoumi memorandum and subsequent civil litigation may draw on overlapping FBI investigative material. The 2025 court's jurisdictional assessment supplies an important judicial evaluation, but does not independently authenticate every allegation in the underlying record.

No publicly reviewed polygraph result establishes the January decision-makers' motives. Nor can a witness's belief about an intelligence operation be treated as equivalent to a contemporaneous operational instruction.

## 5\. Causation: did the withholding help the hijackers remain undetected?

There is a meaningful causal argument, but it must be stated carefully.

Mihdhar and Hazmi used their identities in ordinary transactions, obtained identification and banking services, and maintained a relatively visible presence in San Diego. The DOJ OIG documented these circumstances and identified several opportunities for investigation. Earlier dissemination could have enabled immigration checks, watchlisting and field inquiries. citeturn246203search0

The counterfactual remains uncertain. Earlier FBI awareness would not automatically have produced surveillance, detention, arrest or disruption. Legal thresholds, prioritization, staffing, available leads and the men's subsequent movements all matter.

Moreover, the August 2001 episode demonstrates that **possession of the relevant information did not automatically result in an effective search**\. Once the FBI acquired the travel information, internal restrictions and prioritization problems still prevented timely identification. This limits claims that the January hold alone determined the outcome.

The best-supported causal statement is that the withholding **deprived FBI personnel of opportunities to identify and investigate the men earlier**, contributing to a chain of failures. The evidence does not isolate how much of the ultimate failure was attributable to the original hold rather than later decisions.

## 6\. Original records, acquisition limits and failed searches

| Record or question | Current status | Why it matters |
|--------|--------|--------|
| January 5, 2000 draft CIR and system audit trail | Contents and hold reconstructed by inspectors general; complete original not independently obtained in this review | Confirms exact text, revisions, recipients and authorization |
| January 5 internal CIA cable asserting FBI transmission | Described in DOJ OIG; original not independently obtained | Could resolve whether the assertion was a mistake, assumption or knowingly inaccurate |
| January 13 follow-up and replies | Described in CIA OIG; original exchange not i
````

The intelligence/criminal-investigation separation was invoked, but whether particular nondisclosures reflected policy, misunderstanding or another purpose requires individual examination.




July 4, 2001

	

Mihdhar reentered the United States at JFK using his own identity.

	

The absence of an effective watchlist entry eliminated a potential interception opportunity.




August 21–24

	

FBI analyst Mary located the earlier travel reporting and informed FBI headquarters employee Donna. Immigration checks confirmed Mihdhar's July entry. Watchlisting action followed.

	

Demonstrates that some personnel actively worked to resolve the lead once they recognized its importance.




August 29–September 11

	

The FBI opened a search for Mihdhar, but assigned insufficient urgency and restricted involvement by certain New York criminal investigators.

	

This was a separate FBI prioritization and legal-policy failure after the CIA information became available.

The dates and actor labels above follow DOJ OIG Chapter V, particularly pp. 238–255, 310–350, and CIA OIG pp. 53–55. The March 2000, June 2001 and August 2001 chronology is also described in the 9/11 Commission's Chapters 6 and 8. 
oig.justice.gov
+2

An important correction to overly broad withholding accounts: the FBI did receive some information in January 2000. Its January 5 threat update incorporated portions of the Malaysia reporting, and James documented his FBI briefing. However, the DOJ OIG found no corroboration that the U.S. visa information reached FBI personnel. The distinction is central to assessing whether someone selectively withheld the most domestically actionable information.

The DOJ OIG also identified a separate FBI failure: the limited information received was not entered into a useful, searchable investigative system. That is evidence for a mixed explanation rather than one exclusively attributable to CIA decisions. 
oig.justice.gov
+1

2. Evidence matrix

FBI Executive Order 14040 release, 2021 closure and addendum.

U.S. District Court, ECF 11182, August 28, 2025.

The recruitment allegations deserve scrutiny precisely because they propose a mechanism connecting the January information hold to the San Diego support network. Canestraro's CS-23 alleged CIA operational files, instructions through Saudi liaison personnel, and a recruitment attempt involving Bayoumi and Thumairy. But the publicly available declaration is an account of what a confidential source told the investigator; it does not contain the alleged tasking orders or operational files. 
Wikisource, the free online library
+2

3. Competing hypotheses

H1 — Deliberate withholding to protect recruitment or another ongoing operation

Unproved motive

Strongest corroboration: The January hold, its attribution to a supervisor, the subsequent absence of the visa information from FBI records, and confidential-source allegations of an attempted recruitment operation. The 2017 Bayoumi memorandum makes the proposed Saudi-intelligence intermediary more concrete.

Strongest disconfirmation: Some Malaysia intelligence was shared; an internal CIA cable asserted that the visa information had been passed; and no authenticated CIA recruitment instruction or corresponding operational file was recovered. The people interviewed by the inspectors general did not acknowledge the alleged motive.

Assessment: A serious, testable hypothesis, but the crucial causal link between the hold and an operation is missing.

H2 — Compartmentation and protection of intelligence sources

Plausible

Strongest corroboration: The Malaysia surveillance involved foreign liaison relationships, sensitive collection and compartmented reporting. The later Cole discussions involved restrictions on transferring intelligence to criminal investigators.

Strongest disconfirmation: The January CIR was specifically drafted for FBI dissemination, and its proposed contents included basic visa information. No recovered instruction explains why that information could not be shared with suitable handling restrictions. The January hold and June 2001 restrictions also involved different circumstances.

Assessment: Could explain selected decisions, but a general appeal to source protection is insufficient without identifying the source at risk and the decision-maker's reasoning.

H3 — Mistakes, institutional fragmentation and bureaucratic breakdown

Well supported

Strongest corroboration: Incomplete FBI records, inadequate supervision of detailees, poor tracking of intelligence dissemination, missed watchlisting opportunities, failure to connect the Cole investigation, and insufficient priority during the August search. DOJ OIG identified these as concrete deficiencies.

Strongest disconfirmation: An explicit instruction to hold a prepared report cannot be characterized as a purely accidental transmission failure. Multiple missed opportunities also warrant individual explanations rather than being collectively attributed to bureaucracy.

Assessment: Strongly supported as a contributing explanation. It does not fully explain why the January hold was ordered.

H4 — Deliberate withholding for another intelligence purpose

Unresolved

Strongest corroboration: The hold may have served an operational objective unrelated to recruitment—for example, preserving surveillance, developing a foreign-intelligence lead, or awaiting confirmation of identities and collection.

Strongest disconfirmation: No authenticated record establishes such an objective or shows that the responsible officials believed delaying FBI notification would advance it.

Assessment: An important alternative to recruitment. Intentional nondisclosure does not uniquely identify its purpose.

The hypotheses are not mutually exclusive. A supervisor might have intended to protect sensitive collection, while other personnel assumed dissemination had already occurred, and subsequent FBI failures compounded the original decision.

This mixed explanation is consistent with the established record, although it too remains partly inferential.

4. Individual responsibility and source independence

The strongest documentary attribution concerns Michelle's recorded hold instruction and its attribution to John. The inspector general's labels are pseudonyms, and this assessment does not independently establish the real identities behind them.

The public allegations naming Richard Blee and Tom Wilshire require a separate evidentiary step: matching each person to a particular instruction, document, approval authority and date. A witness's retrospective identification cannot simply be substituted for the pseudonyms in the inspector general's reconstruction. Canestraro's declaration attributes a withholding allegation to CS-16 and a recruitment account to CS-23, but their underlying knowledge and independence have not been established. 
Wikisource, the free online library

There are three particularly important source-independence problems.

First, the 9/11 Commission, CIA OIG and DOJ OIG reviewed overlapping agency records and witnesses. Agreement between their narratives is valuable, but it is not equivalent to three independent contemporary witnesses.

Second, multiple former FBI personnel cited in Canestraro's declaration may have relied on common post-attack investigative information. Their accounts should be compared against the underlying files, not counted as independent confirmation simply because they appear in separate paragraphs.

Third, the 2017 Bayoumi memorandum and subsequent civil litigation may draw on overlapping FBI investigative material. The 2025 court's jurisdictional assessment supplies an important judicial evaluation, but does not independently authenticate every allegation in the underlying record.

No publicly reviewed polygraph result establishes the January decision-makers' motives. Nor can a witness's belief about an intelligence operation be treated as equivalent to a contemporaneous operational instruction.

5. Causation: did the withholding help the hijackers remain undetected?

There is a meaningful causal argument, but it must be stated carefully.

Mihdhar and Hazmi used their identities in ordinary transactions, obtained identification and banking services, and maintained a relatively visible presence in San Diego. The DOJ OIG documented these circumstances and identified several opportunities for investigation. Earlier dissemination could have enabled immigration checks, watchlisting and field inquiries. 
oig.justice.gov

The counterfactual remains uncertain. Earlier FBI awareness would not automatically have produced surveillance, detention, arrest or disruption. Legal thresholds, prioritization, staffing, available leads and the men's subsequent movements all matter.

Moreover, the August 2001 episode demonstrates that possession of the relevant information did not automatically result in an effective search. Once the FBI acquired the travel information, internal restrictions and prioritization problems still prevented timely identification. This limits claims that the January hold alone determined the outcome.

The best-supported causal statement is that the withholding deprived FBI personnel of opportunities to identify and investigate the men earlier, contributing to a chain of failures. The evidence does not isolate how much of the ultimate failure was attributable to the original hold rather than later decisions.

6. Original records, acquisition limits and failed searches

Record or question

	

Current status

	

Why it matters




January 5, 2000 draft CIR and system audit trail

	

Contents and hold reconstructed by inspectors general; complete original not independently obtained in this review

	

Confirms exact text, revisions, recipients and authorization




January 5 internal CIA cable asserting FBI transmission

	

Described in DOJ OIG; original not independently obtained

	

Could resolve whether the assertion was a mistake, assumption or knowingly inaccurate




January 13 follow-up and replies

	

Described in CIA OIG; original exchange not independently obtained

	

Tests whether personnel actively pursued the travelers




March 2000 Bangkok cable and recipient acknowledgments

	

Substantially reconstructed; original routing records not independently obtained

	

Identifies who knew Hazmi had entered the U.S.




June 11, 2001 meeting notes and individual accounts

	

Reviewed through official investigative reconstructions

	

Tests the precise information withheld and the rationale invoked




CIA–Saudi recruitment instructions and Bayoumi operational files

	

Not authenticated or obtained

	

Most direct test of the proposed operational motive




Original records underlying CS-23

	

Not obtained

	

Determines whether the allegation rests on direct knowledge, documentary evidence or hearsay




Complete FBI San Diego source-handling records

	

Not obtained

	

Tests whether actionable observations were available locally




Original CIA/FBI watchlist requests and receipts

	

Not independently acquired

	

Could distinguish failure to initiate from failure to execute




Individual disciplinary and accountability findings

	

Available in part through inspector general reporting

	

Could identify explanations accepted or rejected for specific personnel

These are unresolved acquisition gaps, not proof that the records are nonexistent or withheld for an improper purpose. I did not establish an exhaustive negative search across every National Archives collection, CIA release or FBI Vault production.

The original CIA and DOJ inspector general reports are available, but access to their summaries does not mean the underlying cable traffic, message metadata, interviews and operational files have been independently examined.

7. Records most likely to change the assessment

1. January 5 hold authorization and message history

An authenticated explanation identifying the supervisor, intended duration, reason and operational concern would be the most direct evidence for or against intentional protection of an operation.

2. FBI receipt or dissemination records

A contemporaneous FBI acknowledgment of Mihdhar's visa information, accompanied by the actual document, would materially weaken the claim that the information was withheld from the Bureau. A failed delivery or explicit cancellation would strengthen the narrower obstruction finding.

3. Operational tasking and liaison records

A dated instruction to recruit Mihdhar or Hazmi, connected to the January hold decision-makers, would strongly corroborate H1. A documented unrelated collection objective could instead support H2 or H4.

4. Original witness interviews and contemporaneous notes

These could establish whether the recruitment account rests on direct participation, documentary knowledge, secondhand reporting or retrospective inference.

8. Overall assessment

The evidence supports three conclusions with different levels of confidence.

Established: CIA personnel possessed critical information about Mihdhar in January 2000; a proposed FBI dissemination was affirmatively held; significant visa and travel information did not reach FBI investigators in time; and multiple subsequent opportunities were missed.

Supported but not conclusively attributable: The information failures contributed to the inability to identify and locate the men earlier. Several individual and organizational decisions contributed, including decisions within the FBI after the relevant intelligence became available.

Unresolved: Whether the January hold protected a recruitment effort, another intelligence operation, sensitive collection, or reflected a temporary operational decision that was subsequently mishandled. The current public evidence does not establish the proposed CIA-directed Saudi recruitment operation or a coordinated effort to permit the attacks.

The most discriminating next step is not collecting further general evidence of intelligence relationships. It is establishing a documentary connection between the person who authorized the January hold, the reason for that instruction, and any contemporaneous operational activity involving Mihdhar, Hazmi or Bayoumi.

That connection would materially distinguish deliberate operational protection from the competing explanations. Without it, the evidence supports intentional interruption and consequential intelligence failure, but not an established motive of protecting an ongoing recruitment operation.

No contacts, requests, payments or external transmissions were made.
